# Lessons Learned — Incident Evidence {#doc_lessons_learned_evidence}

**Date:** 2026-08-20
**Purpose:** The cataloged incidents behind `doc/lessons_learned.md`. Every `INC-NNN` identifier cited by a lesson card resolves here, and every row cites the dated `devlog/` entry it was mined from
**Module:** cross-cutting


## What this is

537 incidents mined mechanically from the BELFEM devlog corpus (298 entries) and the
debt register. An incident is an episode with **a wrong belief, a regression, a retraction, a
false positive, a masked defect, a wasted investigation, or a near-miss caught by process** —
not simply "a bug existed".

Mining was done by 15 parallel agents working to one binding brief, each on a disjoint slice
of the corpus, so that no slice's reading could anchor another's. Coverage is mechanical: every
one of the 298 entry files was assigned to exactly one batch, and each miner listed the
entries in its batch that yielded no incident (reproduced at the end of this file).

**Ids.** `INC-001` .. `INC-539`, assigned in batch order at assembly time and **stable**. The
lesson cards in `doc/lessons_learned.md` cite these identifiers and nothing else.

**Cost is `UNRATED` in every row.** The per-thread cost ratings that only the project author
could supply were never provided, so the field is empty throughout and no cost was inferred.
The consequence is that any ranking derived from this catalog degenerates to recurrence — a
measure of how much got written down, not of what hurt.

**Evidence tier.** Everything here is `reviewed`: it is the record of what the corpus says,
re-read at write time. Where a row says an executable gate ran, that is the corpus's claim
about its own past, not a gate the distillation executed. No gate was run to build this file.

## Why the catalog is 4 to 9 times larger than expected

The distillation was designed around a sanity target of 60 to 120 incidents, on the reasoning
that far more would mean ordinary bug reports were being logged as incidents. The result was
**537** on first assembly. Nothing was trimmed to fit the target. What the number appears to
mean:

1. **The corpus is mostly not implementation logs; it is audit and diagnosis logs.** Of the
   298 entries, a large majority are titled as audits, traces, reviews, jury rounds or
   root-cause investigations. An entry whose entire subject is a wrong belief being corrected
   yields at least one incident by construction. Incident-per-entry near 1.0 is therefore
   structural here, not over-mining.
2. **A few entries are long forensic records that genuinely contain many distinct wrong
   beliefs.** `dl20260814_bezier_cp_copper_audit.md` is 462 lines; `dl20260818_doxygen_site_structure.md`
   is 1129. Batch B06 (the currency-sweep thread) returned 68 incidents from 18 entries
   because a sweep's output *is* an enumeration of stale items.
3. It remains possible that some rows are ordinary defects rather than incidents. That is
   measured, not assumed — see the checkpoint below, which classifies a random sample.

**Consequence for Stage 2, and the correction applied:** raw recurrence counts are inflated
by clusters that happen to contain one very productive entry. Stage 2 therefore reports, for
every cluster, **both** its incident count **and** the number of distinct corpus entries those
incidents come from. A cluster of 30 incidents drawn from 3 entries is a different animal
from one of 30 drawn from 25, and only the second is evidence of a recurring pattern.

## Known double-count risk between the register and the devlogs

Closed register rows were excluded from mining precisely to stop recurrence inflation, with an
exception for closed rows whose status cell names no session record — expected to be a short
list. **It is 45 of 50 closed rows** — closure provenance in this
register lives in dated header passes covering groups of rows, not in the rows themselves.
Batch B15 therefore mined far more of the register than the exception envisaged.

Mechanical consequence, measured: **19 debt-register ids are referenced from more than one
batch**, involving roughly 40 rows (7% of the catalog). Some are genuinely distinct
incidents about one id (the defect itself, and separately the register row being wrong about
it); some may be true duplicates. Affected rows are flagged `[register-overlap]` in the citations
column. **Stage 2 assigners are instructed to treat a flagged pair as one incident when the
wrong belief is the same.** Not silently merged here, because Stage 1 does not judge.

## Row schema

`id | thread | dates | symptom | wrong belief at the time | what was actually true | how discovered | cost | fix class | citations | open residual`

Rows marked `**pre-distilled**` in the last column are ones where the corpus stated the
lesson itself, in its own words; there are **73** of them and they carry priority into
clustering.

## Table repairs, recorded rather than hidden

25 rows arrived containing a literal `|` inside a code span or math expression
(`[E|+E]/h`, `` `H|` ``), which splits a markdown cell. These were repaired at assembly by
rejoining the over-split field — 22 in the "what was actually true" column and
3 in the "open residual" column, distinguished by whether the `UNRATED` cost cell
was still in its correct position. No row was dropped and no content was discarded. The
repaired ids are listed at the end of this file.

| INC-101 | T01_ghost_nitsche | 2026-03-19, 2026-04-06, 2026-04-07 | Interface coupling too weak on an HTS/Hastelloy stack; after the penalty was re-scaled, a `1e-3 Ohm m` contact layer stalled near `-40 dB` and a `1 Ohm m` buffer layer produced `NaN`s | First: the hard-coded `eta = 4` was suspected as the cause of weak coupling (the harmonic weighting itself having been "verified against literature" on Day 2). Then: the replacement `alpha = eta*(rho_m/hm + rho_s/hs)` with an absolute floor was believed to be the robust fix | `eta` was not the main problem — with `rho_harm = rho_m*rho_s/(rho_m+rho_s)` the penalty reduces to `eta*rho_HTS/h_HTS`, so it is set almost entirely by the HTS side and raising `eta` only scales it linearly. The uncapped replacement then failed in the opposite direction: for `h = 1e-6 m`, `eta = 4`, metal `rho = 1e-7`, alpha goes from `0.8` (metal-metal) to `≈4.0e3` (1e-3 contact) to `≈4.0e6` (1 Ohm m buffer) while the consistency terms stay weighted by `rho_harm` at the metal scale — a penalty-dominated operator, and a credible cause of the stall and the `NaN`s. | INC-122 | T13_materials | 2026-08-14 | Guarding the discriminant in the new knot-motion `dt/dT` term | first, that `BELFEM_ASSERT( tSqrt > eps )` was a safe guard; then, after the guard was replaced, that `dt/dT` is a genuine branch point where the derivative is unbounded | Codex refuted the guard: at `J = j3` the discriminant reduces to `R²(1/p − 1/q)²` with `p = n−1`, `q = nff`, so it vanishes when `p == q` — **n = 4 at the default nff = 3**, legal under the `n > 1` precondition. The assert would have aborted a legitimate material, and release would have divided by zero. Grok then refuted the *justification* for the replacement: `dt/dT` carries `1/tSqrt` but multiplies a weight derivative ∝ `(1−t)`, and at `p == q` one has `a == b`, hence `1 − t = −tSqrt/a`, so the product is finite — the singularity is **removable**, converging numerically to 6.9858e-3 as J → j3. Codex added a third correction: `tSqrt` is computed before the guard, so a roundoff-negative discriminant yields NaN, and NaN fails every ordinary comparison, so a `tSqrt <= tol` test would be silently bypassed and the NaN would propagate into the tangent; the guard now tests the discriminant with the negated comparison `!( tDisc > tol )` | Two-phase pre-registered protocol: both auditors reviewed the plan and then audited the diff; the NaN bypass was demonstrated with a discriminant of −1e-18 | UNRATED | code | devlog/dl20260813_drhodt_tleg.md:136-167 | Noted in passing and out of scope: the pre-existing `|a|> eps` assert has its own degeneracy at n = 7, where a = 0 exactly — "same class of trap" (devlog/dl20260813_drhodt_tleg.md:142-143) |
| INC-123 | T13_materials | 2026-08-14 | After porting Silver and Indium to the new `Metal::create_cp`, both were badly wrong on first evaluation: Silver had an 18 % discontinuity at `T3` and a *negative* extrapolation slope above it; Indium was +119 % at the bottom of its Bézier range | that the new fits or the new cp construction were at fault — and, earlier, that the cp construction was clean, since the first audit pass on Copper came back with no such finding | `Bezier::xi_by_x` bisects on `[-1.25, 1.25]`, extrapolating the curve 12.5 % past each end before searching. A cubic Bézier only interpolates its control polygon on `[-1, 1]`; outside it, lopsided outer spans fold the curve back, so `x(xi) = X` has **two** roots inside the bracket and the bisection can converge silently on the extrapolated one, with no iteration-count warning. On the Silver high curve (spans 0.766 / 1.880 / **0.038**) every T between 1132 K and 1223 K — all in-domain — acquires a second root, and at `x = X3` both bracket endpoints have the same sign, so the bisection's invariant is void yet it terminates *successfully* at `|f|< 1e-12`. A census found every curve in the tree monotone on `[-1,1]` and **five of seventeen** not monotone on `[-1.25,1.25]`. Copper never triggered it because all six of its spans are balanced — which is why the first audit pass came back clean | Two new material ports produced grossly wrong numbers; numeric replication of the bisection isolated the bracket; a census of every Bézier basis in the tree quantified the blast radius | UNRATED | code | devlog/dl20260814_bezier_cp_copper_audit.md:145-216 | Behavior changes only for out-of-domain queries; the sole ones in the tree are thermal-expansion curves at the top of their spline grid (Copper 2.0 K past the end, alpha change 1.3e-4 relative, at a knot above the melting point) — devlog/dl20260814_bezier_cp_copper_audit.md:208-216. **pre-distilled**: "Metal::create_cp itself queries the exact domain endpoints … Endpoint queries are maximally fold-vulnerable." |

| INC-168 | T14_docs_registry | 2026-08-11 | `make check` came back with two `GesvdWorkBufferReuse` failures on real types only | The test's assertion — that the workspace size LAPACK *queries* meets reference LAPACK's documented minimum `max( 3*mn + max(m,n), 5*mn )` — was believed valid | The test was wrong and the wrapper right about correctness: MKL's `dgesvd` query returns **7** for a 4x3 `'A','A'` problem where the formula gives 15, and MKL completes with `lwork = 7`, `info = 0`. The reference minimum is *sufficient*, not *necessary*. Underneath sat a real defect: the wrapper's acceptance test for a caller-supplied buffer uses the same reference minimum, so the buffer it grows on its own first call (7) is below its own threshold (15) — every later call re-enters the query branch, and across changing shapes the shared buffer *shrinks* (7 → 5). On an EXODUS-ordered tet the naive map is left-handed (`det[e01,e02,e03] = -1` in parameter space); the required map has node 1 carrying zeta. | Christian identified the origin of the trap; Claude confirmed the handedness computation; the fix was made in the generator and the regenerated tables re-pinned, with residual vectors exactly zero under Octave/SymPy and independently reproduced by Christian in MATLAB. A parse-back acceptance gate re-parsed the edited C++ table text into sympy and passed four checks. | UNRATED | code | devlog/dl20260814_tet10_table_fix.md:12-19, devlog/dl20260814_tet10_table_fix.md:21-29, devlog/dl20260814_tet10_table_fix.md:30-50 | **pre-distilled** — "The original defect existed precisely because the generator and the hand-written assembly conventions diverged without a gate between them." Closed at formula level only: "reviewed and symbolically exact, but no compiled binary has run" (devlog/dl20260814_tet10_table_fix.md:52-58). Note: the acceptance gate's own first run produced 1e-15-crumb false violations because the parser read float literals instead of exact rationals (devlog/dl20260814_tet10_table_fix.md:44-49). |

The straight path was masked because `link()` evaluates its Jacobian at evaluation point 0, and with point 0 on the first edge (eta = 0) the crossed entries coincide with the correct values. | The test battery's first full run; Valgrind clean ruled out uninitialized reads, and a private-access probe printing the Jacobian entries showed b=c, e=f, h=i. But `ldb` must come from the allocation, never a formula: `ldb = max(M,N)` satisfies LAPACK's numeric constraint while lying about the buffer, and underdetermined `DGELS` writes rows `M+1..N` of `B`, overrunning a `B` allocated with `M` rows. The Armadillo branch returning `n_cols()` under `'T'` passed a wrong stride for any non-square matrix; the Blaze branch already (correctly) ignored the flag. ## Checkpoint — sample verification

Two samples were drawn and every cited line re-opened and read. **20 checks, 20 CONFIRMED,
0 REFUTED, 0 UNVERIFIABLE.** Both samples are recorded, including the flaw in the first one.

### Sample A — 10 targeted rows (biased; recorded so the bias is visible)

Drawn while mining was still running, by picking the headline incident each miner reported.
**This is not a random sample and it overstates catalog quality**: headline incidents are the
ones a miner examined most closely, so they are the best-supported rows in the batch. It is
kept because it verified 10 load-bearing claims, not because it measures the catalog.

| # | claim checked | verdict | evidence re-read |
|---|---|---|---|
| A1 | default-initializer flip `mCreateGhostFacets` was the regression, with the pre-distilled lesson attached | CONFIRMED | `dl20260423_thinshell_convergence_audit.md:170,182` |
| A2 | `MPI_Abort` truncated 5 of 13 test binaries | CONFIRMED | `dl20260811_ndebug_error_path_tests.md:4,12,26` |
| A3 | a periodic fix "validated" by a run whose deck had no periodic block | CONFIRMED | `dl20260616_periodic_5f_fix.md:67,76` |
| A4 | both fuse flags are false in the current source; Δt collapse reproducer at t=2.0825 ms | CONFIRMED | `cl_ThinShellFactory.hpp:353-354`, `dl20260809_sideconnector_viz_regression_round.md:10,18` |
| A5 | `Bezier::xi_by_x` bisects `[-1.25,1.25]` and can converge on an extrapolated second root | CONFIRMED | `dl20260814_bezier_cp_copper_audit.md:156,159,164-167` |
| A6 | The phantom-dof row retracted; the false premise entered via the brief's "established facts" section | CONFIRMED | `dl20260815_dofcount_jury.md:1,3,10,157` |
| A7 | greg3 false convergence at −124.43 dB, closed at reproducer tier by an A/B | CONFIRMED | `dl20260807_greg3_false_convergence_jury.md:6,28,47,54-55` |
| A8 | the lost t=3100 dump and the standing rule it produced | CONFIRMED | `dl20260819_bnorm_restart_anomaly.md:48-57` |
| A9 | an audit wrote "Verified: edge-key uniqueness holds", later refuted | CONFIRMED | `dl20260717_bfm_load_profile.md:105-108` |
| A10 | MKL `gesvd` query returned lwork 7, below the reference minimum | CONFIRMED | `dl20260813_dr66_gesvd_workbuffer_floor.md:24` |

### Sample B — 10 random rows (the sample that actually measures the catalog)

Drawn with `random.seed(20260820)`, `random.sample(range(537), 10)` — fixed seed, so the draw
is reproducible and could not have been re-rolled after seeing which rows verified.

Drawn: **INC-074, INC-199, INC-256, INC-342, INC-397, INC-399, INC-425, INC-441, INC-471, INC-512.**

| id | verdict | note from re-reading the cited lines |
|---|---|---|
| INC-074 | CONFIRMED | `dl20260407_layered_patch_test_trace.md:171-185` is explicitly headed "ranked candidates, not findings" — supports the row's "held with high suspicion" framing |
| INC-199 | CONFIRMED | `dl20260818_doxygen_site_structure.md:685-706` is headed "A measurement that was wrong"; the omitted `src/linalg/lapack/` is named |
| INC-256 | CONFIRMED | `dl20260811_ndebug_error_path_tests.md:30-33` confirms `USE_DEBUG-MODIFIED:INTERNAL=ON` in a directory named "debug" producing `-O2 -DNDEBUG` |
| INC-342 | CONFIRMED | `dl20260816_bdf_history_restore.md:28-37` — "accused, then acquitted (F7, retracted in phase 3)", true observation with a wrong conclusion |
| INC-397 | CONFIRMED | `dl20260730_cmake_generator_pinning.md:7-20` carries the symptom; the two-generator cause follows |
| INC-399 | CONFIRMED | `dl20260701_cmake_find_package_migration.md:108-127` is the Codex+Grok audit section with post-audit corrections |
| INC-425 | CONFIRMED | `dl20260806_gas_literature_verification.md:128-132` states the double-pv subtraction "is refuted analytically" |
| INC-441 | CONFIRMED | `dl20260701_circuit_restart_plan_and_template.md:24-41` confirms `mTime` private, no setter, cold restart replaying from t≈0 |
| INC-471 | CONFIRMED | `dl20260705_fvm_math_reaudit_and_plan.md:34-37` gives the exact collision: N=6, (4,0) and (3,2) both → 24 |
| INC-512 | CONFIRMED | `todo/debt_register.md:218-221` — all three struck with "premise dissolved" wording |

### What Sample B says about the size deviation

Every one of the 10 random rows carries a genuine wrong belief and its refutation. **None is a
bug report in disguise.** On this sample the spec's suggested explanation for an oversized
catalog — that ordinary defects are being logged as incidents — is **not supported**; the
better explanation is the one given above, that this corpus is predominantly audit and
diagnosis writing, where an entry's subject *is* a corrected belief.

Stated with its scope: this is a 10-row random sample of 537, so it bounds the
bug-report contamination rate loosely, not tightly. It is evidence that contamination is not
the dominant effect, not evidence that it is zero.

## Amendments — corrections recorded in place, never by deletion

### A-1 — INC-130 citation drift, raised by Codex, verified by re-reading

**Raised:** Codex citation audit (Deliverable 2), verdict `UNVERIFIABLE`, on the grounds that
the row's "Iron beta/gamma double-set" claim is supported elsewhere in the same devlog but not
within the cited range.

**Verified independently by re-reading, and Codex is right.** The row cites four ranges in
`devlog/dl20260814_bezier_cp_copper_audit.md` — `:376-406`, `:412-416`, `:358-362`, `:329-330`.
The beta/gamma double-set claim is supported at **`:323-328`**, which begins six lines before
the nearest cited range and is contained in none of them. `:376-406` supports the other
claims in the row (Fe/Ni transport, `rho_0` never set, Nickel `set_RRR`) and does support them.

**Effect:** the row's *substance* stands — the defect is real and documented — but one of its
four claims was not reachable from its citations. The correct anchor is
`devlog/dl20260814_bezier_cp_copper_audit.md:323-328`.

**The row is amended here rather than edited in place**, so the drift stays visible. No card
rests on INC-130 alone; L-14's evidence cites INC-132 and INC-123 for the Bézier material, not
this row, so no Layer 2 claim is affected.

**What it says about the catalog:** exactly what Codex concluded — this is citation drift, not
a taxonomy failure. One row in a 15-row random sample had a range that missed by six lines,
which is consistent with the 20-for-20 result of the orchestrator's own two samples and puts
the observable drift rate somewhere low but non-zero. Stated with its scope: 1 of 15 in
Codex's sample, 0 of 20 in the two samples of this run, so 1 of 35 checked rows overall, and
the one failure was a near miss rather than a fabricated citation.

### A-2 — two incidents this run promised and did not deliver, now added

**Raised:** Grok, "where the catalog is weak" item 6 — that the thread map's own corpus-hygiene
findings do not appear as catalog rows. **Verified, and Grok is right.**

The distillation's thread map stated of those two findings that *"both are carried into Stage 1
as incidents."* They were not. No miner was ever given them, because they were discovered while
partitioning the corpus rather than living inside any one batch's file list. The thread map made a
promise that the mining plan had no mechanism to keep.

The catalog does contain INC-133, the **2026-04-08** instance of the same defect (50 devlogs on
disk, 48 in the index). It does not contain the **present-state** instance, which is the one
that matters, and which is exactly the specimen S13 exists to hold. Added now:

| id | thread | dates | symptom | wrong belief at the time | what was actually true | how discovered | cost | fix class | citations | open residual |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| INC-538 | T14_docs_registry | 2026-08-20 | Eight devlog entries exist on disk and are absent from the index that is supposed to enumerate the corpus | `devlog/README.md` was treated as the index of the corpus, and INC-133's 2026-04-08 fix was treated as having closed the class | Still open and larger: `dl20260324_mesh_element_review`, `dl20260407_layered_patch_test_trace`, `dl20260408_devlog_audit`, `dl20260417_hex8ts_audit`, `dl20260805_tests_old_triage`, `dl20260807_greg3_false_convergence_jury`, `dl20260811_workflow_best_practices_doc`, `dl20260814_doxygen_warning_fixes` are on disk and not indexed. The greg3 entry is a full three-AI jury round with a live reproducer confirmation, not a minor omission | Mechanical diff of `ls devlog/*.md` against the link targets parsed from the index, during Stage 0 of this run | UNRATED | documentation | `devlog/README.md` (291 indexed links vs 298 files on disk); `devlog/dl20260408_devlog_audit.md:20` for the 2026-04-08 precedent | none — a mechanical check in the code-to-document direction would prevent recurrence |

Tycho is a `nonfree/` module and seven tycho devlogs do live in `nonfree/devlog/`, so an entry for proprietary work was indexed in the open-source tree while its content went elsewhere or was never written | `git log --all --diff-filter=D` on the linked path returning nothing, plus `ls nonfree/devlog/` showing the tycho family, during Stage 0 | UNRATED | documentation | `devlog/README.md:211`; `nonfree/devlog/` (7 tycho entries, listing only — contents out of scope by Christian's veto) | none — the nonfree/open-source separation rule is in `CLAUDE.md`; the gap is a link check |

**Effect on counts:** the catalog is **539 rows**, while the clustering and the lesson cards were
computed on 537 and are **not** retro-fitted — they were locked as a pre-registration before
these two rows were added. Both attach to cluster S13, whose card L-05 already rests on 47
incidents across 20 threads; two more change no ranking, no threshold and no card. The
discrepancy is recorded here rather than repaired by rewriting a locked result.

### A-3 — B15 has no coverage section

**Raised:** Grok, item 3. **Verified.** The consolidated `noinc` record for batch B15 reads
`(section missing)`: the debt-register miner did not emit an "entries yielding no incident"
list. For the fourteen devlog batches that list exists and makes the denominator auditable;
for B15 it does not.

This matters more than it would for another batch, because B15 is exactly where over-mining
was predicted in advance — the spec expected a short list of closed rows lacking a session
record and the miner found 45 of 50. **The 42 B15 rows cannot be checked against a stated
denominator.** Not repaired tonight; listed in the handoff as the cheapest single piece of
follow-up.

### A-4 — episode-level duplication is real and larger than the DR-id check found

**Raised:** Grok, items 1 and 5 — that the same *wrong belief* appears as both a devlog row and
a register row, so recurrence counts an episode twice.

**Verified, and the mechanical check in this catalog was insufficient.** The `[register-overlap]`
flag keys on a shared `DR-` id, which misses any pair that does not cite one. Grok named ten
pairs; measuring text similarity across the belief and truth fields marks **8 of 10** as the
same episode, and INC-170/INC-502 — which the metric scored *low* — was confirmed the same
episode by reading both rows: one 2026-08-13 false positive about multi-rank edge coating,
mined once from the sweep devlog and once from the register. So at least **9 of 10 hold**, and
the automated measure understates rather than overstates.

The pairs, as Grok listed them: INC-162/510, INC-166/216/501, INC-155/517, INC-170/502,
INC-145/506, INC-218/533, INC-336/520, INC-360/531, INC-164/526, INC-154/251/522.

**Consequence, stated plainly:** S13 and S2 are the clusters most inflated by this, and they
are precisely the two that a doc-and-register-heavy corpus would inflate. The distinct-entry and
thread-spread counts reported alongside each cluster mitigate but do not remove it, because a
duplicated episode genuinely does come from two different files. **A recurrence count here is a
count of rows, not of episodes**, and should be read that way until the pairs are collapsed.
No card's existence turns on it — L-05 and L-08 would survive a 30% cut to their counts — but
the *ranking* would move.

The level had to be MEASURED (the embedded B = 0 rows), not inferred. The hang it would have caught was real (both jury voices had flagged the original wrapper as an infinite stale-Δt retry). | dl20260829_dr127_certified_exit.md |
| INC-558 | Shared-checkout hygiene, and a signal-selection failure inside it. A session closing out saw `M devlog/README.md`, ran `git diff <file> | grep '^[+-]'`, got no visible output, concluded "touched, no content change", and ran `git checkout --` — destroying another session's index entry for a devlog it did not own. `git diff --stat` on the same file read `1 insertion(+)`: the contradicting evidence was available and the weaker signal was acted on because it agreed with the assumption. Two rules: `git stash` is the reversible form of `git checkout --` and is the correct default in a tree with concurrent writers; and `--stat` is the authority on whether a file changed, not a grep whose output can be swallowed by formatting. The devlog body itself was untouched, so the entry was reconstructable — by a peer session, from the body rather than the header, which produced a better line than the original destroyer's reconstruction. | dl20260830_dr112_and_abort_default.md |
| INC-559 | The clearest correlated-prior failure in the corpus, and the one the cohomology edit ban was built on. Analyzing DR-29, **Claude and Grok independently concluded** the Smith-stage matrix `mD(1)` was missing a phase-2 merge projection — a defect in code that is correct. Codex refuted it with a Schur derivation: `BA=0` forces the pivot-row identity `αd+λD=0`, so the transformed lower operator is `[0; D]` and the live restriction `createMatrixFromCoboundaryMap` computes IS the retracted operator. Grok's alternative fix (switch the matrix source to boundary maps) was discarded at reconciliation. A majority vote would have corrupted a working cohomology kernel; a first-principles derivation by the minority voice saved it. | dl20260830_dr29_coreduce_analysis.md:41-48 |
| INC-560 | An over-generalisation of a published trick, proposed against the omit loop the ban now uses as its canonical example. `coreduceOmit` removes ONE 0-cochain, re-runs the whole `pCoreduce(0..2)` cascade, and repeats — so it removes them all in the end, and batching the omission looks like free speed. Struck as unsafe because batch omission strands 1-cells with empty boundary, and `pCoreduce` only ever pairs a cell whose boundary holds exactly one entry (`cl_SimplicialComplex.hpp:364`), so they survive into cocombine as spurious generators. **A second, subtler instance rides on this row and was only found on 2026-08-31:** the 2026-07-03 devlog justified the strike by saying "Mrozek §5 licenses omitting ONE 0-cell per connected component", and that gloss is wrong. `mrozek2009.txt:444-464` adds ∅ as a simplex of dimension **−1** whose coboundary holds every vertex — one such generator per connected component — so that vertices become free cofaces; it licenses no 0-cell omission, and BELFEM's loop adds no −1 cells. The false attribution propagated unchallenged into `doc/ai_collaboration_protocol.md` §7.1 and into card L-22 two months later, inside a rule whose content is "open the primary source". The *safety* conclusion is BELFEM's own and survives; only the citation was borrowed. | dl20260703_coreduce_risk_reeval.md:25-27; cl_SimplicialComplex.cpp:1236-1257; literature/papers/topology/mrozek2009.txt:444-464 |
| INC-561 | Deviation-from-textbook read as defect, with the significance inverted. The in-tree full-rescan `pCoreduce()` was flagged as a deviation from Mrozek & Batko Algorithm 6.1's worklist. The literature check reversed the framing — the worklist is the baseline and the full rescan is the deviation — and then dissolved the concern entirely: Thm 6.2 guarantees homology preservation for ANY valid removal order, so removal order affects reduction depth and nothing else. Reading the paper both corrected the direction of the finding and cost it its severity. | dl20260703_coreduce_risk_reeval.md:21-24 |
| INC-562 | A prior session's own conclusion reused as a template, and refuted 8 weeks later. The 2026-07-03 round recorded "we already have a worklist in-module", pointing at `pCocombine`. The 2026-08-30 three-way analysis found this is a category error: `Cell::pop` is LIFO, `unique()` sorts the queue, the pop dereference is unguarded, the size-2 test is raw, and there is no ±1 check. Shape matched; semantics did not. Conclusions about unfamiliar algorithms decay into false premises faster than conclusions about familiar ones, because nothing in the reader's prior contradicts them. | dl20260830_dr29_coreduce_analysis.md:53-56 |
| INC-563 | The policy document written to describe this failure mode exhibited it. The cohomology-ban devlog's canonical example — "for cohomology and not for homology, a node is removed so the process can start" — was Gregory's own phrasing and true, but did not match the live `coreduceOmit`, which loops one removal per stall; its supporting example (DR-111 "carried without incident in the plumbing") named a `fem/kernel` thin-shell defect and a routine that lives INSIDE the ban; "Gregory's published work" was a 2025 preprint in preparation; and Layer 1's line count was measured before a second edit and reported wrong in the tripwire file itself. A jury of Codex `gpt-5.6-terra`/high and Grok `grok-4.6`/high found all four. Grok also inverted the true node-removal sentence at high effort with the source open — a sentence a careful auditor inverts is a failed sentence whether or not it is true. | dl20260831_cohomology_ai_edit_ban.md; tmp/ai_exchange/review_cohomology_edit_ban.md |
| INC-564 | The two "AI edits inside the core that held" — and why neither is the counter-instance it first looks like. (a) `759a0801` restructured `pGeneralizedCocombine`; the pre-image was read at ban time and the claim of preserved semantics is true, but the function still carries DR-29 #10's zero-neighbor skip, so the refactor preserved a latent defect it did not understand. (b) DR-107 deleted `Cohomology::clean_greedy()` and the `mFunClean` dispatch — **and this was heavily human-guided surgery, not an AI success story (Christian, 2026-08-31).** Both algorithms are needed: the production path is SPFA *plus* a greedy rectifier, `cohomology::rectify_greedy_sweeps` called from inside `clean_spfa` at `cl_Cohomology.cpp:548`, with `Cohomology::clean()` forwarding to `clean_spfa` at `cl_Cohomology.cpp:189-192`. What was deleted was the standalone member and the function-pointer switch; the greedy ALGORITHM stayed, because a human who knew that steered the cut. Framed by an AI on its own — "two cleaners, one is dead" — the plausible move is to delete the greedy algorithm and take the rectifier with it. The lesson is not "AI edits here can be safe" but **"they were safe when a human supplied the constraint the code does not state"**. | 759a0801; dl20260825_dr107_homology_cleanup.md; cl_Cohomology.cpp (rectify_greedy_sweeps inside clean_spfa) |
